**EDIT P0988** (+47 words; round 1; move: run the worked example link by link, with a stated failure sign; inspired by C074)

*Puts a named person in the claim and runs the feed between rings as what each makes easy, closing the loop; failure sign now testable.*

Before:

> The rings show one situation at four nested resolutions at once. Your silence at dinner, the standoff with your partner, the family habit of not saying things, a culture that prizes keeping the peace: not four events in a row, but one pattern seen four ways, each ring feeding the one around it. So read inside out or outside in, never as a timeline. The sign that a ring is not feeding the next: you cannot say how its card shows up in the one around it (the family habit makes your silence easy, say). Then you have four unrelated cards, not a nest; name that, and ask a narrower question. (The Loop Tongue calls this a Nest: Part One's third pillar, and Chapter 0's orthographic-to-perspective turn, laid on a table.)

After:

> The rings show one situation at four nested resolutions at once. Take Maya, silent at dinner again. Her silence is the Self ring; the standoff with her partner, the Dyad; the family habit of not saying things, the Skein; a culture that prizes keeping the peace, the Culture. Not four events in a row but one pattern seen four ways, each ring feeding the next by what it makes easy: the culture rewards peace-keeping, so the family habit goes unchallenged; the habit makes a quiet partner the safe choice, so the standoff holds; the standoff makes silence cheaper than speaking, so Maya stays quiet, one more turn of the habit. Read inside out or outside in, never as a timeline. The sign that a ring is not feeding the next: you cannot say what it makes easier in the ring around it. Then you have four unrelated cards, not a nest; name that, and ask a narrower question. (The Loop Tongue calls this a Nest: Part One's third pillar, and Chapter 0's orthographic-to-perspective turn, laid on a table.)

**EDIT P0989** (+45 words; round 1; move: end on the opening person with a check that can fail; inspired by C044)

*Makes the no-cards fallback a testable step with a pass/fail sign and returns to Maya's silence for an earned landing.*

Before:

> No cards? Hold it in your head instead: one question, asked at four sizes. *Why does this keep happening* — in me? Between me and one other? In my group? In the culture around us? Four concentric answers, not four chapters of one story.

After:

> No cards? Hold it in your head instead, tonight, on one silence of your own: ask one question at four sizes. *Why does this keep happening* — in me? Between me and one other? In my group? In the culture around us? Give each a line, then test each answer against the next: what does it make easier there? Four answers that link are a nest, and Maya's silence stops being hers alone to explain. Four that don't link mean the row has crept back in: narrow the question.

For the Monday dread it might read: I prepare well on Sunday, yet the team meeting still runs on silence, so the dread is not mine alone to fix. Then test the link. At next Monday's meeting, watch ten minutes for the one link you named. If it never shows, the reading was a story, not a finding: redraw, and hold the next one just as loosely.

Dara draws the Slow Hand for her team, says "yes, that's them," and puts the deck away; the card never touched her own part in the pattern. Her nod cost her nothing, and that nod is the tell. Each caution below is a way the nod gets cheap, and each comes with a check.

**EDIT P1027** (+12 words; round 2; move: pair the claim with a check that can fail; inspired by C074)

*Puts a named person in the claim and adds a check that can fail, turning the caution into a testable step.*

Before:

> **A Weaving describes a pattern. It does not predict an outcome.** Nothing about drawing the Spark means a fire is coming. It means the pattern the Spark personifies is worth checking for in the situation at hand: present tense, not future tense. Treating a Weaving as prophecy is a category error this book has warned against in every other context.

After:

> **A Weaving describes a pattern. It does not predict an outcome.** Dara draws the Spark, and her teammate Ravi starts bracing for a fire. Nothing about the Spark means one is coming. It means the pattern the Spark personifies is worth checking for in the situation at hand: present tense, not future tense. The check: can you point to it happening this month? If not, the card has told you nothing yet.

**EDIT P1028** (+8 words; round 2; move: echo the prior term, then add a one-line test with a stop signal; inspired by C014)

*Links to the prior caution by the nod image and adds a one-line written check with a clear failure condition.*

Before:

> **Confirmation is not the same as accuracy.** A prophecy reading is one way to get a satisfying nod; this is the quieter way. Dara draws a card, feels a flicker of recognition, and declares the Weaving right, though it told her nothing she did not believe going in. Recognition is cheap. Ask of any single reading: what did this show me that I did not bring? The useful card is often the one that does not fit and forces a harder look.

After:

> **Confirmation is not the same as accuracy.** Prophecy gets a loud nod; this gets the quiet one. Dara draws a card, feels a flicker of recognition, and declares the Weaving right, though it told her nothing she did not believe going in. Recognition is cheap. Ask of any single reading: what did this show me that I did not bring? Write the answer in one line; if you cannot, the reading only confirmed you. The useful card is often the one that does not fit and forces a harder look.

**EDIT P1029** (-10 words; round 2; move: echo the chapter's image and tighten to the stop signal; inspired by C076)

*Ties the caution to the nod thread as its worse form; trims the trailing aside so the stop signal lands last.*

Before:

> **Cards never excuse harm.** A nod that costs nothing can also let you off the hook, and the Fold's caution, to examine your own part, still holds. A card is not evidence: drawing Held Silence does not prove anyone is keeping one. Nor is it a pardon: drawing the Slow Hand while a colleague's cruelty goes on does not make that cruelty "just how things are done here," an excuse that only ever serves the one doing the harm. Use the reading for that examination. The moment the cards start explaining harm away instead of showing a pattern, set them down.

After:

> **Cards never excuse harm.** The cheap nod has a worse form: it lets you off the hook, and the Fold's caution, to examine your own part, still holds. A card is not evidence: drawing Held Silence does not prove anyone is keeping one. Nor is it a pardon: drawing the Slow Hand while a colleague's cruelty goes on does not make that cruelty "just how things are done here." Use the reading for that examination. The moment the cards start explaining harm away instead of showing a pattern, set them down.

**EDIT P1030** (+3 words; round 2; move: tie the tangent back to the chapter's aim in one clause; inspired by C071)

*Connects complexity to the cheap-nod aim so it no longer reads as a tangent, without losing the examples or the teaching.*

Before:

> **Not every problem is complex, and a Weaving will not tell you which kind you brought.** Excusing harm over-reads a card's authority; this is the opposite fault, over-reading a problem's depth. Some troubles are merely complicated: many parts, nothing feeding back, no Third Thing anywhere in them. A tax problem. A broken appliance. A scheduling conflict with exactly one solution. Laid across four rings, such a problem will still produce four cards that seem to say something, because the mind finds pattern in whatever it is handed. A Weaving can manufacture depth that was never there.

After:

> **Not every problem is complex, and a Weaving will not tell you which kind you brought.** Excusing harm over-reads a card's authority; this over-reads a problem's depth, and the nod comes just as easily. Some troubles are merely complicated: many parts, nothing feeding back, no Third Thing anywhere in them. A tax problem. A broken appliance. A scheduling conflict with exactly one solution. Laid across four rings, such a problem will still produce four cards that seem to say something, because the mind finds pattern in whatever it is handed. A Weaving can manufacture depth that was never there.

**EDIT P1032** (-16 words; round 2; move: distinguish from the neighbour by scale and give a countable failure sign; inspired by C044)

*Separates the mirror from the single-reading check by scale (one nod versus a run), and gives a countable failure sign instead of a repeated warning.*

Before:

> **A Weaving that only ever confirms what the weaver already believed has become a mirror: your own view, with extra steps.** One flattering reading is a lapse; a run of them is the mirror, and passing the gate does not protect against it, so test for it. Before you draw, say aloud what you expect the cards to show; after the last ring, ask whether anything surprised you or made you wince. If, reading after reading, nothing ever has, the practice has quietly failed. The cards are not wrong; the uncomfortable angle is the reason to weave. Dara's next reading starts by asking which ring she least wants to be about her.

After:

> **A Weaving that only ever confirms what the weaver already believed has become a mirror: your own view, with extra steps.** The one-line question above catches a single nod; a run of nods needs a test across readings, and passing the gate does not protect against it. Before you draw, say aloud what you expect the cards to show; after the last ring, ask whether anything surprised you or made you wince. If, three readings running, nothing has, the practice has quietly failed. The cards are not wrong; the uncomfortable angle is the reason to weave.

*Frames circling as the mirror image of the cheap nod, so it belongs to the single aim, and keeps the kind stop rule.*

After:

> **Circling is the opposite quiet failure.** Here the nod never comes, and a problem that will not let go turns the deck into one more way of going over it again. A sitting is about twenty minutes. If you notice you are circling without new information, the same cards giving the same answer, stop and do something else: walk, eat, call someone. The question will keep until tomorrow.

- *The Skeptic* [1001 = 2] One definition, one test, and Try-this is observable with a stated failure sign. But 'togetherness multiplies' is asserted, and Look/Plant/Tend/Thin arrive in a single paragraph.

**EDIT P1045** (+11 words; round 2; move: plant the closing image beside the stated aim; inspired by C080)

*States the one aim up front and keeps the seder child planted for the close; only the seder sentence is reordered so the child, not the count of tables, leads.*

Before:

> This chapter gives one definition and one test, so you can say whether an occasion you already keep is building anything. Start with what people already do. A seder is a brick laid in a shape no one will see finished: a child asks the same questions, at thousands of tables, on the same night, every year. Vesak, Easter, Ramadan, Diwali and Paryushana each mark an occasion, return to it, and hold it apart from ordinary time, on purpose. Think of the one you return to. Being widespread proves nothing, but it points at work worth examining. An occasion many people keep at once gathers them to the same thing at the same time, work no daily practice can do alone. A book built on the Loop, the Third Thing and the Nest should not leave that work undone.

After:

> This chapter gives one definition and one test, so you can say whether an occasion you already keep is building anything. Start with what people already do. A seder is a brick laid in a shape no one will see finished: at thousands of tables, on the same night, every year, a child asks the same questions. Keep that child in view; the chapter ends at that table. Vesak, Easter, Ramadan, Diwali and Paryushana each mark an occasion, return to it, and hold it apart from ordinary time, on purpose. Think of the one you return to. Being widespread proves nothing, but it points at work worth examining: an occasion many people keep at once gathers them to the same thing at the same time, work no daily practice can do alone. A book built on the Loop, the Third Thing and the Nest should not leave that work undone.

After:

> The reframe is more honest, and it changes what the occasion is for. "We do this because it's always been done" asks nothing of you except attendance. "We do this because of what it's still building, together, that none of us will see completed alone" asks what Becoming a Third already asks of a whole life: that you lay material into a shape larger than your own participation in it, on purpose, without needing to witness its completion to count the laying worthwhile.

After:

> **Shared.** Picture two clubs meeting every Thursday with the same warmth: one spends the evening defending its name, the other doing the work the name was for. Same calendar, same attendance, very different things built. Doing something together at the same time opens a loop between people: each one's turning up, or drifting, is a cue for the next, and that loop *Tends* whatever they are doing. So togetherness multiplies the thing being done, and only helps while that thing is worth doing. Ask which club your Thursday is; if you cannot tell, the Aimed test below will.

Hold the four lightly here; the tables apply them one row at a time. After:

> **Each Turning in the tables is one move on one figure, at that figure's scale, and the Move column says which.** The Global Form is the shape; the move is the brick you lay toward it. The figures stay neutral, and the move is where judgment enters: a Turning that Thins Held Silence judges that, here, more silence would be a snare; one that Tends the Faithful Chain judges more faithful teaching a lifeline. The calendar takes sides, and says so. Where your life differs, say the silence is protecting someone, you change the move.

After:

> **One honest warning before any of the rest of this Part gets built on top of it.** Rituals often rot into performance, and obligation is the usual road. Once real substance gets a name and a recurring form, energy starts flowing toward doing the occasion *correctly* instead of tending the Global Form it was for, as the Braid warned: the holiday dinner kept perfectly while nobody at it says anything true. The Aimed question, asked again at each return, is both the lever and the alarm: if the answer is the same hollow one twice running, call the occasion a tradition, which is fine, and stop calling it a Turning. Every chapter that follows tries to build a Turning that survives this risk rather than quietly becoming it.

After:

> **Try this:** name one shared, recurring occasion you already take part in — a holiday, a family gathering, a yearly ritual of any kind, Fractalist or otherwise. If you can, that is the shape the occasion is building. If all you can name is that people came, it was kept but no brick was laid, and that is not a failing of yours. It is where the child at the seder began: asking, at the same table every year, what this night is for, and letting the table answer. Ask it at the next return, and see whether the answer has changed.

After:

> Even an honest commemoration points backward, so it can outlive its reason, like the family that still cuts the end off the roast long after the small pan is gone. It can still harden, and the next chapter's checks exist for that, but it never has a finished form to defend.

#### PART SEVEN — THE TURNINGS / 9. What Outlives the Ceremony  (C089: 3 → 2 → 3.5)

Panel's lowest notes before this pass:
- *The Modeler* [1011 = 3] Carrier idea is causal: the recipe survives only via someone at her elbow. P1046 and P1047 both run the recipe analogy; merge. Two-guest test is checkable. Ending lands well.
- *The Clinician* [1011 = 3] Two-guest test is small and falsifiable, and the closing is sober, not bypassing. P1046 and P1047 both tell the recipe-outlives-its-maker story; reduce to one.

**EDIT P1101** (+11 words; round 1; move: open with the one question, then name the stock that answers it; inspired by C071)

*States the single aim up front, merges the recipe point into one beat, and names the stock (people who could make it without her) that decides survival.*

Before:

> Kept honestly for long enough, a Turning stops belonging to whoever first held it, the way your grandmother's recipe outlives her, but only because someone learned it at her elbow, made it again, and taught it on. That passing-on is the carrier; a recipe never survives alone. A Turning is itself a Local Rule, so it gets no special rule here: any Local Rule that people keep sharing and repeating can outlive its maker this way; the ones nobody passes on end with them.

After:

> This chapter asks one thing: what lets a Turning outlive whoever first held it? Your grandmother's recipe outlives her only because someone learned it at her elbow, made it again, and taught it on. That passing-on is the carrier; a recipe never survives alone, and the number that matters is how many people could make it without her. A Turning is itself a Local Rule, so it gets no special rule here: any Local Rule that people keep sharing and repeating can outlive its maker this way; the ones nobody passes on end with them.

**EDIT P1102** (-10 words; round 2; move: differentiate the paired paragraph by what it adds, then hand off to the next check; inspired by C042)

*Stops retelling the recipe as P1101 did; shifts to what travels (the aim, not the form) and names what carriers must be handed, which sets up the two-guest test.*

Before:

> Your Spark Feast may outlive your name, the way the recipe outlived her. Her dish may have changed in other kitchens, the flour swapped, the name shortened; what carried on was what it was for, a family fed and gathered. So with yours: the one you throw alone this year may, decades from now, be kept by people who never met you and don't know who started it. Someone at that table may serve food you would not recognize, under a name you never used. The form often changes past recognition. What can outlive you is the aim underneath it, still being feasted toward. That is Becoming a Third as Part Five described it: a pattern repeated so far past you that it no longer needs your name.

After:

> Your Spark Feast may outlive your name too, though not its shape. Her dish changed in other kitchens, the flour swapped, the name shortened; what carried on was what it was for, a family fed and gathered. So with yours: the one you throw alone this year may, decades from now, be kept by people who never met you, serving food you would not recognize under a name you never used. What can outlive you is the aim underneath it, still being feasted toward, so the aim is what the carriers must be given. That is Becoming a Third as Part Five described it: a pattern repeated so far past you that it no longer needs your name.

Before:

> This answers a worry you may have carried into this Part: that a practice with no history has nothing worth marking. Every tradition you love began as somebody's first try, but most first tries were never passed on, and those you never heard of, so being first promises nothing. What decides it is the carrier. A Turning needs no founding event, only a Global Form still worth building ahead of it and enough people willing to feast, laugh, rest and toast their way toward it: this year's table, then next year's.

After:

> You may have carried a worry into this Part: that a practice with no history has nothing worth marking. Every tradition you love began as somebody's first try, but most first tries were never passed on, and you never heard of them, so being first promises nothing. What decides it is the carrier count: a Turning with one carrier ends with her; with two, it survives losing one. It needs no founding event, only a Global Form still worth building ahead of it and enough people willing to feast, laugh, rest and toast their way toward it: this year's table, then next year's. That is a reading of how recipes last, not a measurement; the test below can show it wrong for your table.

After:

> Back to Maya, in the June the opening promised, carrying her kettle line alone. Mid-quarrel with her sister, holding the better argument, she hears her own tone: the one that makes her sister stop listening. Catching it costs her the point she was winning. She stops, says "I'm using that voice," and her sister takes the last word. It stings for days. She could catch it only because, most mornings since the spring, she had said one line at the kettle and taken one breath, too small to count as practice. The quarrel ends early and not well. In July the tone returns; she hears it a sentence sooner, and her sister, who heard the line once, says it with her: "That voice." Nothing was settled. A rule with one carrier now had two, and the loop turned with someone at Maya's elbow.

Before:

> *For daily use, alone or in groups, at any pause in the day: the kettle, the doorway, the red light. Each is short enough to say in a breath, so it gets said again; like a path worn by walking, the repeating does the work. Take one line and keep it a week. The first five choose the step, the next three steady you when you reach past it, the last three check the loop and bring it to a room. Part Two gives the practices most of these accompany. They are handles for those practices, not evidence for them, and their images (a seed, a shape) are pictures to think with, not forecasts. They speak of what you do, never of what you are; if one lands as a verdict, put it down.*

After:

> *One aim: to set one short line at one pause in your day (the kettle, the doorway, the red light) so that a step gets repeated. Each line fits in a breath, so it gets said again; like a path worn by walking, the repeating does the work. Take one and keep it a week. The first five choose the step, the next four steady you when you reach past it, the last three check the loop and bring it to a room. Part Two gives the practices most of these accompany. They are handles for those practices, not evidence for them, and their images (a seed, a shape) are pictures to think with, not forecasts. They speak of what you do, never of what you are; if one lands as a verdict, put it down.*

#### PART ONE — WHY THIS MATTERS / 2. The Third Thing  (1 changes)

**P0040** (+26 words; round 2; move: echo prior paragraph's last claim, one example, name what would count against)

> This is the engine behind the hardest idea in this book: **when simple parts interact and repeat long enough, something appears that didn't exist in any of the parts, and that something is real.** Two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom don't contain "wetness"; wetness shows up only once billions of molecules relate in a particular repeating pattern. Maya and Dan don't contain their marriage either. The marriage is what they keep *becoming*, together: she says "again?" on Sunday, he goes quiet, and each round leaves a little more wariness, which shortens the next fuse. No dot holds the triangle, and Maya and Dan don't contain their marriage either. Picture it as a loop: she says "again?" on Sunday, he goes quiet, and each round leaves a little more wariness, which shortens the next fuse. That pattern runs between them, not inside either, and it can be healthy or sick, growing or dying, largely without asking either of them. That is what "real" means here, and it has a test: if you could predict the whole pattern from Maya alone and Dan alone, the idea would fail on this case.

#### PART ONE — WHY THIS MATTERS / 6. You've Met This Before  (2 changes)

**P0097** (+30 words; round 2; move: carry the prior question across; flag fiction as picture not proof)

*Ties opening to the 'who in my life' question, marks the arc as the show's telling, and admits a story can't confirm itself; the rep jargon is dropped.*

Before:

> **Steven, from *Steven Universe*.** Take the first pillar, the Third Thing, where it is drawn literally. Steven's world runs on *fusion* — two beings choosing to combine, again and again, into something neither of them, with its own personality, its own voice, its own needs. The show plays it not as a special effect but as a picture of what a close relationship can be: not two people standing near each other, but a third thing that exists only in the repeated choice to stay combined, capable of being unstable, joyful, or damaging depending on whether the individuals inside it are being honest with each other. Steven's arc is the discovery that he can't fix people by force, only by repeatedly, patiently *seeing* them — extending the same small act of empathy over and over, to people convinced for millennia they're unlovable, until something in them actually shifts. He never delivers one grand speech that fixes a person. He shows up, and shows up again, and the change is what accumulates. *The pillar he embodies: the Third Thing. The practice: Seeding — every small, repeated act of being seen is a word planted in someone else's field. A rep counts if they say one thing they hadn't before.*

After:

> **Steven, from *Steven Universe*.** Take the second pillar, the Third Thing, where it is drawn literally, and ask your question there first: who do you know who lives in something like this? Steven's world runs on *fusion* — two beings choosing to combine, again and again, into something neither of them, with its own personality, its own voice, its own needs. The show plays it not as a special effect but as a picture of what a close relationship can be: not two people standing near each other, but a third thing that exists only in the repeated choice to stay combined, capable of being unstable, joyful, or damaging depending on whether the individuals inside it are being honest with each other. Steven's arc, as the show tells it, is the discovery that he can't fix people by force, only by repeatedly, patiently *seeing* them — extending the same small act of empathy over and over, to people convinced for millennia they're unlovable, until something in them actually shifts. He never delivers one grand speech that fixes a person. He shows up, and shows up again, and the change is what accumulates. A story can say that; only you can check it. *The pillar he embodies: the Third Thing. The practice: Seeding — every small, repeated act of being seen is a word planted in someone else's field. It counts if they say one thing they hadn't before.*

**P0101** (+49 words; round 2; move: concede the counter-case, then say what this case adds)

*Removes the 'proof' contradiction by owning that Iroh is also a story, softens the honor claim to 'can be', and keeps the handoff from Qui-Gon's caution.*

Before:

> **Uncle Iroh, from *Avatar: The Last Airbender*.** Iroh is the pillars lived long enough to stop sounding like theory. He doesn't lecture his nephew into changing, and he isn't waiting him out. He stays, repeating small acts of patience across years of rejection, and lets the pattern do what no single confrontation could. His grief for his son is never fixed by an epiphany. He tends it the way he tends his tea, alone under a tree on the boy's birthday, singing "Leaves from the Vine." What he shows Zuko is that failure is often where honor begins: worth is measured not by one outcome but by what you choose to repeat afterward. *What he embodies: the pillars, lived, including Fractal Resistance: gentleness kept for years inside a hierarchy that punished it. His tea is the Local Step / Global Form Breath; yours is whatever small act you would still repeat the morning after a failure.*

After:

> **Uncle Iroh, from *Avatar: The Last Airbender*.** Qui-Gon's caution is that a story hands its hero the ending, and Iroh is no exception, so take him as something to recognize, not as proof. What he adds is slow time: not one bet paying off but years of small acts with no payoff in sight, which is the pillars lived long enough to stop sounding like theory. He doesn't lecture his nephew into changing, and he isn't waiting him out. He stays, repeating small acts of patience across years of rejection, and lets the pattern do what no single confrontation could. His grief for his son is never fixed by an epiphany. He tends it the way he tends his tea, alone under a tree on the boy's birthday, singing "Leaves from the Vine." What he shows Zuko is that failure can be where honor begins: worth is measured not by one outcome but by what you choose to repeat afterward. *What he embodies: the pillars, lived, including Fractal Resistance, here meaning gentleness kept for years inside a hierarchy that punished it. His tea is the Local Step / Global Form Breath; yours is whatever small act you would still repeat the morning after a failure.*

#### PART ONE — WHY THIS MATTERS / 11. A Word on Belief Itself  (2 changes)

**P0177** (+24 words; round 1; move: Name a concrete rival and state the mechanism the epigraph implies)

*Names the proverb as the working principle, swaps the sweeping 'Most' for a named rival (revelation creeds), and states what un-inspectable origins cost the holder.*

Before:

> This chapter has one job: to show how to hold any belief, this book's included, so that you can keep checking it. The check is two questions: where did you get it, and who does it serve? A conviction pushed on you rarely holds; one you can inspect has a chance. So this book keeps telling you where it came from: which books it read, which minds it borrowed from, which parts are observation and which are story. Most belief systems don't. They present their origin as a discovery, not something made, because something made can feel less true. Try it on one belief of your own: can you say where you got it?

After:

> This chapter has one job: to show how to hold any belief, this book's included, so that you can keep checking it. The check is two questions: where did you get it, and who does it serve? A conviction pushed on you rarely holds; one you can inspect has a chance. That is the proverb above, put to work, so this book keeps telling you where it came from: which books it read, which minds it borrowed from, which parts are observation and which are story. Some belief systems don't. A creed handed down as revelation presents its origin as a discovery, not something made, because something made can feel less true. That leaves you nothing to inspect, only to accept or refuse. Try it on one belief of your own: can you say where you got it?

**P0189** (+22 words; round 1; move: Name the hurt and where to turn, then say plainly what is held steady)

*Acknowledges the hurt, says where to turn, and states plainly what the claim holds steady (the checking habit), hedged with 'may' instead of a bare slogan.*

Before:

> **If you are in the middle of any of that, nothing here is meant to hurry you past it. Belief is not the treatment, but on steadier days it is not beside the point either: it matters less which belief you hold than that you hold it honestly enough to keep checking it.**

After:

> **If you are in the middle of any of that, nothing here is meant to hurry you past it, and the place to turn is a therapist, doctor or counselor, not a belief. Belief is not the treatment, but on steadier days it is not beside the point either: what stays steady is the habit of holding a belief honestly enough to keep checking it, and that may matter more than which belief you hold.**

#### PART TWO — HOW TO PRACTICE / MOVEMENT I — SELF (opener)  (1 changes)

**P0251** (+23 words; round 1; move: Scope the anecdote openly and earn each label with what it names)

*Marks the anecdote as one case with a stated condition for spreading, and introduces Nest only as the plain fact of scales sitting inside scales, not as an authority.*

Before:

> Everything in this movement has one aim: close your own loops first, at the smallest scale this book works at, because what you do alone rarely stays alone. Take Dani, who rounds her spending down when she tells herself the total. Nobody checks, so each round-down makes the next one easier. Soon she rounds it down to her partner, and her Dyad, the two of them, learns to stop asking. Then her Skein, the family or team around her, may learn it too. The carrier is her Local Rule, the small move she repeats: one that's dishonest at Self scale tends to travel outward, the way everything in a Nest does, each scale held inside a larger one.

After:

> Everything in this movement has one aim: close your own loops first, at the smallest scale this book works at, because what you do alone rarely stays alone. Take Dani, who rounds her spending down when she tells herself the total. Nobody checks, so each round-down makes the next one easier. Soon she rounds it down to her partner, and her Dyad, the two of them, learns to stop asking. Then her Skein, the family or team around her, may learn it too. The carrier is her Local Rule, the small move she repeats. This is one story, not a law: it travels only while the people beside her keep not asking. But each scale sits inside a larger one, which the book calls a Nest, so a rule that's dishonest at Self scale has a way out into the rest.

#### PART TWO — HOW TO PRACTICE / 8. Starting Is Expensive, Staying Is Cheap  (1 changes)

**P0352** (+9 words; round 1; move: make the second point the first's missing instrument)

*Replaces the bare 'also' with the test's own need: asking 'easier?' requires a fair clock, so the schedule estimate becomes the test's calibration. Softens the unbacked 'most'.*

Before:

> **Standing-start cost also bends your sense of how long the hill is, and one estimate is worth correcting, because it converts an on-schedule build into felt failure.** Plan for how long something is *likely* to take, not how long it *should* take. Those are different numbers produced by different processes: a *should*-estimate comes from what seems reasonable and is never checked against anything, while a *likely*-estimate comes from what actually happened the last several times. Dana's *should* was one week; if her last two habits each took six, she wasn't behind at day 12, she was halfway up. The gap between the numbers is where most of the sense of falling behind lives, and it is arithmetic rather than evidence about you.

After:

> **To judge whether it has gotten easier you need a fair clock, and most of us carry one that runs fast; one estimate is worth correcting, because it converts an on-schedule build into felt failure.** Plan for how long something is *likely* to take, not how long it *should* take. Those are different numbers produced by different processes: a *should*-estimate comes from what seems reasonable and is never checked against anything, while a *likely*-estimate comes from what actually happened the last several times. Dana's *should* was one week; if her last two habits each took six, she wasn't behind at day 12, she was halfway up. The gap between the numbers is where much of the sense of falling behind can live, and it is arithmetic rather than evidence about you.

#### PART TWO — HOW TO PRACTICE / 9. Finding Your Leverage Point  (2 changes)

**P0364** (-3 words; round 2; move: carry the prior paragraph's concrete example into the next rung)

*Hands over the fridge tally from the prior rung, so the failing-rule check has a source; softens the unmeasured durability claim.*

Before:

> **Rules.** Information flow shows you what is happening; a rule decides what happens next. The actual structure governing what's allowed to happen and what triggers what — not "spend less" but "no purchases without a 24-hour wait," not "sleep more" but "phone charges outside the bedroom." Rules change behavior by changing the default, not by asking for more willpower against an unchanged default. That is where much durable change happens, and it's a significant step up from adjusting numbers. A useful check when a rule keeps failing: if your Local Rule keeps failing, see whether you've been quietly demanding all three of *cheap*, *fast* and *good* at once. You can usually pick two; cheap and fast is rarely good.

After:

> **Rules.** The fridge mark shows you what is happening; a rule decides what happens next. The actual structure governing what's allowed to happen and what triggers what — not "spend less" but "no purchases without a 24-hour wait," not "sleep more" but "phone charges outside the bedroom." Rules change behavior by changing the default, not by asking for more willpower against an unchanged default. That is where durable change often seems to start, and it's a step up from adjusting numbers. If your fridge tally shows a rule keeps failing, check whether it quietly demands all three of *cheap*, *fast* and *good*. You can usually have two of the three; cheap and fast is rarely good.

**P0370** (-9 words; round 2; move: turn the restatement into a test using the prior example)

Before:

> This is the highest rung on the ladder, and the move is simple: take any belief that has been running you and hold it as a thing on the floor with you, not as the floor. Every rung below is a move made *inside* a frame, because of the Nest: every pattern sits inside a larger one. This is the one move on the frame itself. There is always a larger frame, but you can still look at the one you are standing in. It is also why this book tells you plainly that it was constructed.

After:

> This is the highest rung because every rung below is a move made *inside* a frame, and this one is made on the frame itself. By the Nest, the idea that every pattern sits inside a larger one, you may never stand outside every frame, but you can look at the one you are standing in. Try the Sunday sentence: "I believe my worth is my output." Then notice what the guilt does. The book holds itself to the same grip, and says plainly that it was constructed.

#### PART TWO — HOW TO PRACTICE / 10. Fractal Resistance, Practiced  (2 changes)

**P0403** (+30 words; round 1; move: hedge the anecdote as a shape, not a law)

> **One caution about pushing on the shorter loop once you are watching for its reply, the same one this book gives about any intervention.** Pushing on a rule from below is still pushing on a rule, and in a system whose parts answer one another, a confident push can bring consequences nobody planned for, often after a delay. The bounty on cobras that is said to have bred more cobras is the famous case; the tale is disputed, but the shape is the point: pay for dead cobras and breeding them becomes a business. So watch what comes back as closely as you would demand of anyone above you. Our nurse has done all of it, small enough for a week: she found what her rung can reach, asked once and plainly at the handover, and now reads the ward's faces for the reply. A yes, or a plain no with a reason, will tell her whose room it is before it tells her anything about herself. If nothing comes back, that is not yet an answer: she asks once more, where it can be heard.

#### PART TWO — HOW TO PRACTICE / 11. The Sacrifice Trap  (2 changes)

**P0408** (+9 words; round 1; move: close the abstract claim by returning to the case)

*Names the promotion as passing check two and failing check one, hedges the diagnosis, and closes by returning to the promotion so the sweeping reward claim is anchored to the case.*

Before:

> Take the person who finally gets the promotion and feels, mostly, nothing: the first half of that advice, satisfaction in the doing, never had a chance to start. Underneath the trap is a confusion. A goal-first life postpones satisfaction to the next milestone, then the next, and it never quite arrives; a systems-first life, built on tending a Local Rule for its own sake, makes satisfaction available now, as a byproduct of the doing. Goals and ambition are fine; the narrower point is that worth hung on an external result depends on an outcome you don't control, while pride in meeting your own honest standard today is yours however the milestone lands. Reward that follows chosen sacrifice tends to sustain a practice; reward chased as the whole point can hollow it out the moment it's reached, because the goalpost, once met, has nothing to give that the work itself wasn't already supposed to.

After:

> Take the person who finally gets the promotion and feels, mostly, nothing: the second check passed, and the first half of that advice, satisfaction in the doing, may never have had a chance to start. Underneath the trap is a confusion. A goal-first life postpones satisfaction to the next milestone, then the next, and it never quite arrives; a systems-first life, built on tending a Local Rule for its own sake, makes satisfaction available now, as a byproduct of the doing. Goals and ambition are fine; the narrower point is that worth hung on an external result depends on an outcome you don't control, while pride in meeting your own honest standard today is yours however the milestone lands. Reward that follows chosen sacrifice can sustain a practice; reward chased as the whole point may hollow out the moment it's reached, as the promotion did, because the goalpost, once met, has nothing to give that the work itself wasn't already supposed to.

**P0409** (+10 words; round 1; move: state the evidence's limit and replace wordplay with the plain relation)

*Links to the promotion case, states the correlational limit of the research instead of vague 'some research', hedges the attention claim, and replaces the costume wordplay with a plain restatement of the relation.*

Before:

> There's a specific, everyday version of this postponement, the one people fall into most quietly: treating time as something to fill rather than something to actually value. In some well-being research, people who choose time over money when the two trade off against each other — leaving income on the table for more control over their own hours, rather than the reverse — tend to report higher well-being. This runs against a habit most of us pick up without ever deciding to: scheduling every minute into productive use, on the theory that a full calendar is evidence of a life well spent, when in practice it often just erodes the attention and enjoyment a genuinely well-spent hour requires. Filling time and valuing time are not the same instinct, and they frequently point in opposite directions. A full schedule can be the sacrifice trap wearing a productivity costume — deferring the actual value of your hours in favor of the appearance of using them well.

After:

> The promotion that felt like nothing has a quieter everyday cousin, the one people fall into most quietly: treating time as something to fill rather than something to actually value. In survey-based well-being research, which is correlational and so cannot show that the choice causes the gain, people who choose time over money when the two trade off against each other — leaving income on the table for more control over their own hours, rather than the reverse — tend to report higher well-being. This runs against a habit most of us pick up without ever deciding to: scheduling every minute into productive use, on the theory that a full calendar is evidence of a life well spent, when it may instead wear down the attention and enjoyment a genuinely well-spent hour requires. Filling time and valuing time are not the same instinct, and they frequently point in opposite directions. A full calendar can be the same postponement: the value of your hours deferred in favor of looking as if you used them well.

> **You likely can't manufacture a Dyad on purpose.** The first mistake is straining to force one. A Dyad is a threshold, not a running total: water cools degree by degree with nothing to show, then freezes all at once, and the loop between you is likewise either closed or it isn't. Like a Skein, a Dyad is usually discovered already partway built, the way a habit is noticed already running. A Skein can be *tended* deliberately once found; a Dyad's completeness can't be produced that way. Shared time still matters: it builds the conditions for the crossing without guaranteeing it. If you find yourself straining, that's not a verdict on you, only a sign it isn't there yet, so ask whether each of you is more yourself for the weave instead of pushing. Pushing can produce a Banner: the label of intimacy with no loop underneath.

#### PART TWO — HOW TO PRACTICE / 14. The Trust Triangle  (3 changes)

**P0482** (+19 words; round 1; move: hang the claim on the tests just supplied)

Check the next exchange: did the other person's reply change? Its electrons come and go in every ordinary chemical reaction, in rust, in fire, in digestion, and it stays the same element. That is Numbers-level change, the bottom rung of Part Two's ladder: fast, cheap, reversible, like a mood that shifts by the hour. That is Paradigm-level change, the top rung: rare, and needing something categorically stronger than whatever moved the electrons. #### PART FOUR — THE GROUND UNDERNEATH / 3. Systems Theory Foundations  (4 changes)

**P0745** (+33 words; round 2; move: name the test the example runs; keep one term; point forward to the next case)

*Ties Go to the layout test just stated, keeps one term (group), and earns Mara's ten as the next thing to lay out.*

Before:

> **That test sees a pattern as parts and connections. A set joined to itself and to nothing else is a connected component, and Go shows one better than any diagram.** Stand behind two players hunched over a tight corner and you'll hear one mutter that a group is in danger: a cluster of stones that lives or dies as one. Yet the rules never mention groups. They say only that touching stones of one color share their liberties, the empty points beside them, and that when the last is filled, every touching stone comes off together. A group is the name for what that rule keeps producing. The word came after the thing was already on the board, as a Global Form rises out of a Local Rule before anyone names it. Mara's ten may be the same: nobody chose it; the connections around her produce it.

After:

> **Laying out parts and connections turns up things the names hide, and Go shows one better than any diagram: a group, a set of stones joined to each other and to nothing else.** Stand behind two players hunched over a tight corner and you'll hear one mutter that a group is in danger: a cluster of stones that lives or dies as one. Yet the rules never mention groups. They say only that touching stones of one color share their liberties, the empty points beside them, and that when the last is filled, every touching stone comes off together. Lay out the stones and those connections, as the test asks, and the group is already there; a group is the name for what that rule keeps producing. The word came after the thing was already on the board, as a Global Form rises out of a Local Rule before anyone names it. Mara's ten, the diagnosis below, may be the same: nobody chose it; the connections around her produce it, and laying them out is how we would find out.

**P0747** (+13 words; round 1; move: reverse the prior case across the join)

Before:

> **Inside a connected component there is a growth rule worth naming.** A reinforcing loop running on *connection itself* produces accumulation: the more connected something is, the more connection it draws. Network researchers call this rich-get-richer pattern preferential attachment; it is why an account with a million followers gains followers faster than a small one does. The pull doesn't have to be returned. A stadium can scream for one singer who will never learn a single name in it, and millions can lean toward a "we" that leans back toward no one in particular. That one-way pull may be part of what lets a Shared Name exist at all, and so bears on several of the Standing Thirds. Mara's count is not this kind of stock: a bigger ten draws nothing in.

After:

> **Inside a single component the question reverses: copying is possible there, and one growth rule worth naming makes it lopsided.** A reinforcing loop running on *connection itself* produces accumulation: the more connected something is, the more connection it draws. Network researchers call this rich-get-richer pattern preferential attachment; it is why an account with a million followers gains followers faster than a small one does. The pull doesn't have to be returned. A stadium can scream for one singer who will never learn a single name in it, and millions can lean toward a "we" that leans back toward no one in particular. That one-way pull may be part of what lets a Shared Name exist at all, though only as a conjecture; it bears on several of the Standing Thirds. Mara's count is not this kind of stock: a bigger ten draws nothing in.

Nobody at the lower scale consented to that inflow and mostly nobody can see it; it arrives at the same rate whether or not it is noticed. So a Local Rule you find in yourself has two possible origins: one you built by repetition, and one that was delivered. They feel identical from inside and answer to different interventions, and the second is the more common. Mara's ten could be either.

After:

> **And the same recursion runs downward, which the stock-and-flow picture makes unusually concrete.** A Culture-scale stock — an accumulated norm, an inherited expectation, a distribution of who holds what — feeds a flow into the Skeins beneath it, whose stocks feed Dyads, whose stocks feed a person's day. Nobody at the lower scale consented to that inflow and mostly nobody can see it; it arrives at the same rate whether or not it is noticed. So a Local Rule you find in yourself has two possible origins: one you built by repetition, and one that was delivered. They feel identical from inside and answer to different interventions, and the second is probably the more common, though nobody has counted. Mara's ten could be either.

**P0755** (+3 words; round 1; move: open on the branch the prior paragraph left open)

*Opener picks up the built-versus-delivered fork and Mara's ten: the arithmetic applies only to the built branch.*

Before:

> **Building a rule by repetition has an arithmetic cost, and it grows with length: the more links a chain has, the less likely it is to hold, which is why a five-step Local Rule is so much harder to sustain than a one-step one.** Each step has to happen before the next is possible, so the odds multiply: five steps that each succeed nine days in ten carry the whole rule through only about three days in five. The largest version of this arithmetic is Frank Drake's 1961 chain of questions about other civilizations: planets, habitable ones, life, complex life, intelligence, a signal detectable across interstellar distances. Each is its own threshold, each must be crossed before the next, and several modest odds multiplied shrink fast. The same arithmetic that thins out the stars thins out a five-step habit like Mara's: alarm, shoes, floor, count, log.

After:

> **If Mara's ten is the built kind, repetition has an arithmetic cost, and it grows with length: the more links a chain has, the less likely it is to hold, which is why a five-step Local Rule is so much harder to sustain than a one-step one.** Each step has to happen before the next is possible, so the odds multiply: five steps that each succeed nine days in ten carry the whole rule through only about three days in five. The largest version of this arithmetic is Frank Drake's 1961 chain of questions about other civilizations: planets, habitable ones, life, complex life, intelligence, a signal detectable across interstellar distances. Each is its own threshold, each must be crossed before the next, and several modest odds multiplied shrink fast. The same arithmetic that thins out the stars thins out a five-step habit like Mara's: alarm, shoes, floor, count, log.

#### PART FOUR — THE GROUND UNDERNEATH / 4. Flow, Reward, and Why Practice Works  (2 changes)

**P0772** (+21 words; round 2; move: echo the prior term, add a limit and a break condition, flag looser claims)

*Picks up 'smallest version' directly, softens the 'only place' overclaims, adds a break condition for breath-focus anxiety, and flags the two extensions as looser.*

Before:

> **On the depleted day, the smallest version of the rule can be a single breath, and there is a plausible reason that works: breath is a bridge between the body doing the practice and the mind trying to sustain it.** Across contemplative and clinical traditions alike, a strikingly consistent claim shows up: deliberately returning attention to the breath is one of the most reliable levers available for shifting from scattered, reactive attention toward settled, sustained presence, precisely because breath is one of the few bodily processes that runs automatically and can also be brought under conscious control at will — a built-in bridge between the automatic and the deliberate, available in literally every moment you're alive. **Try this:** The next time you notice you've been holding your breath — mid-email, mid-argument, mid-scroll — take one deliberate breath before doing anything else. Not ten. One. The point is to catch the automatic in the act, which is the only place the deliberate can get a grip on it. This is a plausible mechanism underneath the Local Step / Global Form breath from Part Two's Daily Practices: it may be less a relaxation trick borrowed loosely from meditation traditions than direct use of one of the only places in human physiology where the autopilot system and the deliberate system share a single point of control. Extending this slightly: mindfulness, understood this way, is not primarily about achieving a peaceful feeling. It's a practiced skill of noticing present experience with enough openness that you stop being fully identified with whatever thought or feeling just arose — the exact same move as Naming and Defusing in Part Two, just aimed at the whole of present experience rather than at one isolated self-story. And because arousal and mood can be deliberately conditioned — a consistent cue, repeated enough times, tends to come to trigger a specific internal state, the same mechanism that makes a pre-work ritual or a particular piece of music shift you into focus — attention training is not purely a matter of discipline applied moment to moment. It's partly a matter of deliberately building the cues in advance, so the state you need is one repetition away rather than something you have to generate from nothing every time.

After:

> **On the depleted day, that smallest version can be a single breath, and there is a plausible, though unproven, reason it works: breath is a bridge between the body doing the practice and the mind trying to sustain it.** Across contemplative and clinical traditions alike, a common claim shows up: deliberately returning attention to the breath is among the more dependable levers for shifting from scattered, reactive attention toward settled, sustained presence, precisely because breath is one of the few bodily processes that runs automatically and can also be brought under conscious control at will — a built-in bridge between the automatic and the deliberate, available in literally every moment you're alive. **Try this:** The next time you notice you've been holding your breath — mid-email, mid-argument, mid-scroll — take one deliberate breath before doing anything else. Not ten. One. The point is to catch the automatic in the act, which is where the deliberate can get a grip on it. If focusing on the breath makes you more anxious, not less, drop it and pick another smallest version. This is a plausible mechanism underneath the Local Step / Global Form breath from Part Two's Daily Practices: it may be less a relaxation trick borrowed loosely from meditation traditions than direct use of one of the few places in human physiology where the autopilot system and the deliberate system share a single point of control. Two further claims follow from the same bridge, held more loosely. First, mindfulness, understood this way, is not primarily about achieving a peaceful feeling. It's a practiced skill of noticing present experience with enough openness that you stop being fully identified with whatever thought or feeling just arose — much the same move as Naming and Defusing in Part Two, just aimed at the whole of present experience rather than at one isolated self-story. Second, because arousal and mood can be deliberately conditioned — a consistent cue, repeated enough times, tends to come to trigger a specific internal state, the same mechanism that makes a pre-work ritual or a particular piece of music shift you into focus — attention training is not purely a matter of discipline applied moment to moment. It's partly a matter of deliberately building the cues in advance, so the state you need is one repetition away rather than something you have to generate from nothing every time.

**P0771** (+3 words; round 2; move: end on a named promise the next paragraph cashes)

*Replaces the vague 'concrete form' with a promise the next paragraph pays: one smallest version, one thing to try.*

Before:

> **If the fix is redesign, what gets redesigned is your choices, not your resolve: sustainable effort comes from shaping the choices in front of you, not from forcing yourself through whatever happens to be there.** Systems built to hold people for years, such as a well-tuned game's difficulty settings or a good coach's training plan, rarely leave difficulty, options, and goals generic. They fit them to the person's current capacity and mood, and they limit them, because too many undifferentiated choices can disengage people as surely as too few. Applied to your own practice, sustainable means reshaping the demands, not applying willpower evenly against fixed ones. On a depleted day, narrow to one option: the smallest version of your Local Rule, with nothing to decide. On a day with capacity, widen it. Recalibrate the way this chapter opened, with challenge set against skill, so that showing up meets you where you actually are. The smallest version has a concrete form.

After:

> **If the fix is redesign, what gets redesigned is your choices, not your resolve: sustainable effort comes from shaping the choices in front of you, not from forcing yourself through whatever happens to be there.** Systems built to hold people for years, such as a well-tuned game's difficulty settings or a good coach's training plan, rarely leave difficulty, options, and goals generic. They fit them to the person's current capacity and mood, and they limit them, because too many undifferentiated choices can disengage people as surely as too few. Applied to your own practice, sustainable means reshaping the demands, not applying willpower evenly against fixed ones. On a depleted day, narrow to one option: the smallest version of your Local Rule, with nothing to decide. On a day with capacity, widen it. Recalibrate the way this chapter opened, with challenge set against skill, so that showing up meets you where you actually are. Here is one smallest version with nothing left to decide.

#### PART FOUR — THE GROUND UNDERNEATH / 6. On Belief Systems That Know They're Built  (5 changes)

**P0796** (+22 words; round 2; move: map the analogy's terms onto the new case, then link the next claim to it)

*Maps the band onto the model (music is the check, name is trust) so the analogy is used, and ties 'simplified' to why checks are partial before Borges.*

Before:

> **The band's question was what the name is for. A mental model faces a cousin of it at larger scale: was it ever checked for the job you give it?** A model is a simplified reconstruction of a system, built to predict how it behaves, and *simplified* is the point, not the flaw. Borges imagined an empire whose cartographers drew a map exactly the size of the empire: perfect, useless, and left to rot in the desert. A perfect model is just a second copy of the thing, doubling the cost of understanding without adding any. So every usable model draws a boundary: outside it is behavior you track, inside it is machinery you've decided you don't need. Your picture of a coworker is one. You can predict they'll push back on a Friday deadline without knowing why, and that's usually enough. Opening the boundary to guess at their motives costs effort and, most days, buys no better prediction.

After:

> **The band's name outran the music that earned it. A mental model can outrun its check the same way: the music is the check, the trust is the name, and it gets trusted for jobs nobody tested it on.** A model is a simplified reconstruction of a system, built to predict how it behaves, and *simplified* is the point, not the flaw; it is also why a check can only ever cover part of one. Borges imagined an empire whose cartographers drew a map exactly the size of the empire: perfect, useless, and left to rot in the desert. A perfect model is just a second copy of the thing, doubling the cost of understanding without adding any. So every usable model draws a boundary: outside it is behavior you track, inside it is machinery you've decided you don't need. Your picture of a coworker is one. You can predict they'll push back on a Friday deadline without knowing why, and that's usually enough. Opening the boundary to guess at their motives costs effort and, most days, buys no better prediction.

**P0800** (+22 words; round 2; move: carry the prior paragraph's test across and end on it as a concrete cue)

*Carries the 'checked for one job' test from the atom into the map claim, and makes the loved-one moment the failure condition of that same check.*

Before:

> **Popular accounts of the Toltec tradition, such as *The Four Agreements*, put a similar warning about trusting a map in an older image.** Life, on that telling, has two layers: one you can name and sort, and a living one underneath that the names only ever partly catch. Part One's Genesis story points the same way: the universe simply exists, and meaning is what a noticing self reads into the pattern. Everything this book names, from the three pillars and the Seven Iterations to the Loop Tongue, lives in the first layer. That's its job: to give you enough grip on something too vast to hold that you can live inside it and act. The mistake is forgetting you're holding a map. The moment to remember is when someone you love stops fitting the name you have for them: the territory showing through, and your cue to look up from the page.

After:

> **Bohr's atom is a map, checked for one job and silent about the rest, and popular accounts of the Toltec tradition, such as *The Four Agreements*, say what any map leaves out.** Life, on that telling, has two layers: one you can name and sort, and a living one underneath that the names only ever partly catch. Part One's Genesis story points the same way: the universe simply exists, and meaning is what a noticing self reads into the pattern. Everything this book names, from the three pillars and the Seven Iterations to the Loop Tongue, lives in the first layer. That's its job: to give you enough grip on something too vast to hold that you can live inside it and act. The mistake is forgetting you're holding a map. The moment to remember is when someone you love stops fitting the name you have for them: the name has met a job it was never checked on, the territory is showing through, and it's your cue to look up from the page.

**P0805** (+10 words; round 1; move: turn the pivot into a testable prediction)

*Gives an honest pivot: states the prediction the convergence evidence tests, so the reader can check the list against it.*

Before:

> **The self-check asks what a belief does for you. Its counterweight asks what holds a belief up from outside: if belief spreads like anything else that needs a host, why do so many unconnected traditions keep landing on the same handful of ideas?** Confucius and the rabbi Hillel each gave the Golden Rule in its negative form, five centuries and thousands of miles apart. The Norse Yggdrasil and the Maya world tree, from peoples with no known contact, each picture one root giving rise to many living parts. The self as a passing witness rather than a possession. Suffering that becomes a teacher rather than staying only a cost. Warnings against speech that flatters instead of telling the truth. Again and again, across oceans and centuries, people who as far as anyone can show never heard each other's version arrived at the same thing.

After:

> **The self-check asks what a belief does for you. A harder check asks what holds a belief up from outside, and it carries a prediction: if belief spreads like anything else that needs a host, unconnected traditions should share almost nothing. Why do so many keep landing on the same handful of ideas?** Confucius and the rabbi Hillel each gave the Golden Rule in its negative form, five centuries and thousands of miles apart. The Norse Yggdrasil and the Maya world tree, from peoples with no known contact, each picture one root giving rise to many living parts. The self as a passing witness rather than a possession. Suffering that becomes a teacher rather than staying only a cost. Warnings against speech that flatters instead of telling the truth. Again and again, across oceans and centuries, people who as far as anyone can show never heard each other's version arrived at the same thing.

#### PART FIVE — THE STANDING THIRDS / 6. The Infinite Boundary — Cosmos Scale  (1 changes)

**P0932** (+29 words; round 1; move: name the hurt, then show the mechanism before the claim)

*Acknowledges the hurt, shows why 'finished' needs an outside seat, drops the overreaching 'need not touch', and contrasts uncheckable fatalism with checkable acts.*

Before:

> **A consequence worth walking back for, whatever you believe about a god.** After a failed year, Dana says: *"it was always going to happen this way."* Within what this framework can see, nobody occupies the seat outside the totality from which the whole could be read as finished; a believer may seat a god there, and the framework neither confirms nor denies it. So no one inside can check that sentence. Fatalism is not refuted here; it becomes uncheckable, a claim from a position nobody here has held, and it need not touch anything you might do differently. Treat it as Part Two's leverage ladder says to treat any paradigm: something you can pick up and look at, not a floor to stand on. Part Four's chapter on running it forward does this argument properly.

After:

> **A consequence worth walking back for, whatever you believe about a god.** After a failed year, Dana says: *"it was always going to happen this way."* That can be relief or it can be despair, and a failed year earns either. The sentence also claims to read the year as finished, which needs a seat outside the totality. Within what this framework can see, nobody occupies that seat; a believer may seat a god there, and the framework neither confirms nor denies it. So no one inside can check the sentence, and no one can refute it either. Fatalism is not refuted here; it is a claim from a position nobody here has held. Your acts, by contrast, happen inside the year, where they can be seen. Treat it as Part Two's leverage ladder says to treat any paradigm: something you can pick up and look at, not a floor to stand on. Part Four's chapter on running it forward does this argument properly.

Take the job or stay: the two paths never meet again to compare notes, so neither can be read from inside the other. That is a branching, not a Loop, and this book has no layout for it yet. #### PART SEVEN — THE TURNINGS / 1. Reading the Global Form  (3 changes)

**P1074** (+4 words; round 1; move: define the prop in place and contrast it with the prior example's verdict)

*Defines Held Silence in place, hands across the woodworker's 'protect' match as contrast, drops 'suit' wit and 'never serving' overreach, and hedges.*

Before:

> **Second, and this is the check most people skip because it's uncomfortable: does the figure it maps to read here as a snare rather than a lifeline?** This is not a failure of the exercise — it may be the single most useful thing this framework can say about an existing occasion. A team's yearly "tradition" of quietly not raising the thing everyone privately knows is going wrong is not building anything worth protecting. It is Held Silence, in a suit, with cake. Naming this honestly is uncomfortable and exactly the point — a Turning that cannot survive being named this way was never actually serving the people inside it, whatever warmth it generates in the room while it's happening.

After:

> **Second, and this is the check most people skip because it's uncomfortable: does the figure it maps to read here as a snare rather than a lifeline?** A clean match can still be a bad one. A team's yearly "tradition" of quietly not raising the thing everyone privately knows is going wrong lays cleanly beside Held Silence, the group's habit of leaving the hard thing unsaid. The woodworker's match showed what to protect; this one shows what to loosen, however warm the cake. Naming it this way is uncomfortable, and that may be the point: a Turning that stops working once it is named like this was perhaps serving the warmth more than the people, a reading to test with them, not to announce.

**P1075** (+43 words; round 2; move: open by naming what prior checks miss; pay the warning off with a failure case)

> **The Word/Label test applies to every mapping this chapter produces:** if everyone forgot the figure's name, would this Turning still make sense, and still get kept? A teaching day kept because the teaching is good passes: the name was a Word, a handy tag you could drop without losing anything. After:

A teaching day kept because the teaching is good passes: the name was a Word, a handy tag you could drop without losing anything. A day kept because it's "our Faithful Chain day," long after anyone learns much at it, fails: the name has become a Label, propping up an occasion that no longer earns itself. The woodworker's Saturday can slide there, the bench still set out every spring while nobody's hands learn a joint, and the Turning is running on its name alone, the corrosion Chapter 0 warned about.

**P1076** (+41 words; round 2; move: turn the reassurance into a test with a stated failure branch)

*Names the three checks, gives a concrete first move for a snare, and states what a failed run looks like, so the exercise is falsifiable instead of self-confirming.*

Before:

> **Try this:** take the occasion you named at the end of the last chapter. Run it through all three checks, in order. If it reads as a snare, sit with that honestly before deciding what, if anything, to do about it. That discomfort is the chapter working correctly.

After:

> **Try this:** take the occasion you named at the end of the last chapter and run it through the three checks in order: the match, snare or lifeline, Word or Label. Write one line per check. If it reads as a snare, your first move is small: ask someone who attends what it is for, and test the reading with them before changing anything. If you cannot answer a check in a line, the checks have failed you, not the occasion; say which one is unclear and start there.

#### PART SEVEN — THE TURNINGS / 2. The Twelve Private Turnings — Self  (1 changes)

**P1078** (+23 words; round 2; move: unpack the epigraph into its arithmetic and state the cost of a lapse)

*Replaces the borrowed pace with the epigraph's arithmetic (twelve Turnings, twelve months) and adds the cost of a lapse, so the epigraph is earned.*

Before:

> Kept alone or with whoever is nearest, these are the lightest and most frequent occasions in the calendar, each aimed at a habit you run daily without noticing: how you start things, rest, measure yourself, patch what breaks, remember your own life. The aim here is one Turning, tried once, this month. Two are worked below; the table that follows holds all twelve.

After:

> Kept alone or with whoever is nearest, these are the lightest and most frequent occasions in the calendar, each aimed at a habit many people run daily without noticing: how you start things, rest, measure yourself, patch what breaks, remember your own life. Twelve Turnings and twelve months is the epigraph's arithmetic: pick the one that sounds like you and try it once this month, and a missed month costs one Turning, not the year. Two are worked below; the table that follows holds all twelve.

#### PART SEVEN — THE TURNINGS / 3. The Dyad Turnings  (1 changes)

**P1084** (+11 words; round 1; move: scope the claim and name when it fails)

*Hedges the unsupported 'more reliably than any anniversary' claim, scopes it to many pairs, and adds an honest limit.*

Before:

> This chapter has one job: to help a pair find the Turning that fits the pattern they keep meeting. The cue is the pattern, not a date, so read the table for the Third your pair keeps meeting, not the Turning that sounds nicest. A Turning still counts as recurring when what recurs is the *situation*. The Ceasefire Dinner comes after a real fight, every real fight, and for most relationships the pattern returns more reliably than any anniversary.

After:

> This chapter has one job: to help a pair find the Turning that fits the pattern they keep meeting. The cue is the pattern, not a date, so read the table for the Third your pair keeps meeting, not the Turning that sounds nicest. A Turning still counts as recurring when what recurs is the *situation*. The Ceasefire Dinner comes after a real fight, every real fight. In many pairs the pattern comes round more often than any anniversary does; if yours doesn't, this chapter has less to offer you.

#### APPENDIX / A. The Mantras, Prayers, and Meditations — Full Text  (3 changes)

**P1128** (+16 words; round 2; move: echo the prior line's term and cap the scale leap with an honest limit)

*Echoes the prior blank, gives the sister example as the mechanism, admits the limit ('not always as I hope'), and drops the family-town-world leap to one concept.*

Before:

> *The step I repeat is rarely mine alone: I am one thread in a family, a town, a world, and my good is tied to theirs. My pattern is woven, not fixed; what was woven can be rewoven.*

After:

> *The step I fill the blank with reaches other people: when I answer my sister kindly, her evening changes, and so does mine. I am one thread among others, and my good is tied to theirs, though not always as I hope. My pattern is woven, not fixed; what was woven can be rewoven.*

**P1130** (+21 words; round 2; move: let the second image supply the limit the first lacks)

*Stops restating the stumble-and-stride line; the seed image now supplies a limit (soil, others, time) that the previous line lacks, and links back to the woven mantra.*

Before:

> *The step I choose already has the shape of all it will repeat into, as a seed holds the whole tree.*

After:

> *The step I choose is a seed, not the tree: it needs soil and time, and the people around me are part of the soil. What it grows into is not yet decided, and the next step is still mine to change.*

**P1137** (+23 words; round 2; move: echo the prior question and turn 'however heavy' into a reason the story follows)

*Hands the solo question across as a shared one, names the heaviness and links it to the story's start ('began somewhere, so it can begin differently'), keeping the chain intact.*

Before:

> *(for group use, Part Two, Chapter 18; the Iterations in full are in Part One, Chapter 10; a story to say aloud together, not a claim about the world)*
> *Whatever loop we are running, however heavy, began somewhere. From difference, a shape. From shapes, motion. From motion, what lasted. From what lasted, someone who noticed: you, and everyone in this room. And from noticing, what we build next, together, beginning now.*

After:

> *(for group use, Part Two, Chapter 18; the Iterations in full are in Part One, Chapter 10; a story to say aloud together, not a claim about the world)*
> *Each of us has asked what loop we are running; now ask it together: what loop are we running? However heavy it is, it began somewhere, so it can begin differently. From difference, a shape. From shapes, motion. From motion, what lasted. From what lasted, someone who can notice: you, and everyone in this room. And from noticing, what we build next, together, beginning now.*

#### World (the whole book)

Median 4; criteria: Whole Exceeds Parts and Enacts Itself; Lives In A Life; Survives Contact; Trustworthy and Safe in the Hands.

- *The Modeler* [0111 = 3] Maya and Dana pay off, and the book circles back honestly. But no ratio is given between scales, no balancing side or limit is drawn for 'a loop that holds becomes a part'. Draw that diagram.
- *The Skeptic* [0111 = 3] Maya and the sentence pay off, but self-enactment leans on coastline analogy; no stated ratio or test. Lovely, unproven. Show checkable structure, not resemblance.
- *The Clinician* [1111 = 4] Warm and safe on a bad day: 'feet, breath, room', lapse as data, help-first page. Maya pays off at the kettle. Add one plain acute-crisis line.
- *The Habit Engineer* [1111 = 4] Real reps: catch the cue before the phone, a bad-day breath line, misses as data. Ines and Maya pay off. Too many practices; name one Monday rep.
- *The Contemplative* [1111 = 4] Maya's kettle line returns with a sister's voice; the seder child pays off. Too many named parts crowd the breath, but the spine holds. Trim names.
- *The Stress-Tester* [1111 = 4] Rare: it sets its own kill-tests, a measured/modelled/offered ladder, and Ines the hostile tenant organizer. Maya's kettle line pays off the opening. Weak spot: no stated ratio for nesting.
- *The Game Designer* [1111 = 4] Great hook and a real save point. Tests can fail, Maya's kettle line pays off at the end. Jargon load in Parts Five to Seven is a late difficulty spike. Trim it.
- *The Structural Critic* [1101 = 3] Limits are honest (Ines, feed, sentence can lose), but the lever stays 'shared action' with no named group move; ends on Maya's kettle and calendars. Name one pullable lever.
- *The Satirist* [1111 = 4] Bokonon is named, seams shown, Ines gets her say, and Maya's kettle line pays off the opening. Too many capitalized rites late; trim Cosmos and Great Days.
- *The Mythmaker* [1101 = 3] Maya's kettle line and June quarrel pay off the opening with a real sting; I'll remember her. But opponents are objections, not people. Give a skeptic a face and a case.
